When she missed a major deadline for a gallery collaboration she had proposed herself and found she could no longer bring herself to open her project planning software, her mother and a close friend from the Farmhouse Museum both expressed concern and helped her schedule an appointment at a local clinic, where she described feeling unable to handle how thrown off course she felt by the job situation.\n- Chief Complaint: After losing the job opportunity I was counting on, I feel like I’ve been reacting badly to everything and can’t cope with what’s happening in my life.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response): Ever since the job at the nonprofit fell through, it’s like I’ve spiraled—small things like an email from my landlord or a text about a possible gig make my chest tighten, I shut my laptop, cancel plans, and just lie there crying or scrolling on my phone instead of dealing with anything.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- within_3_months_of_stressor: All of this really started in the weeks right after they told me the full-time position was off the table and the grant was gone—I was doing okay before that, and then within a month or so I just couldn’t handle even simple things.\n- within_6_months_after_stressor_or_consequences_end: I keep telling myself that once I either sort out a stable job or decide on a different plan, this reaction can’t go on forever; it feels tied to everything being up in the air right now rather than some long-standing issue.\n- functional_impairment: Because I’ve been so overwhelmed and avoidant, I’ve lost out on paid gigs, stopped showing up to my regular improv group, and even let my lease and loan emails pile up to the point where I’m worried I might miss payments or not have a place lined up to live.\n- duration: It’s been about two months now, ever since they told me the nonprofit job wasn’t happening, that I’ve been feeling this unable to cope and derailed.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

It just feels like everything has been completely derailed and I don't know how to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally enjoy since this job opportunity fell through?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I actually backed out of organizing the Harvard summer fair and completely stopped going to my regular improv meet-ups, which I used to love. Now, instead of doing any of that, I just find myself canceling plans and lying around.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to get that job, or feeling guilty about not being able to cope with the loss of this opportunity?\n\nPatient: I don't think I could have done anything differently, since the organization abruptly lost its funding and froze all hiring. I don't really feel guilty, but I just feel completely overwhelmed and frozen by my inability to cope with everything being thrown off course.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling overwhelmed and disconnected from your usual activities since the job opportunity fell through?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, ever since I found out the nonprofit job wasn't happening. All of this really started within a few weeks of getting the news about the hiring freeze, and I've felt unable to cope ever since.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of being overwhelmed and disconnected from activities have changed or evolved over these past two months, have they been steadily present or have you noticed any fluctuations?\n\nPatient: It has been pretty steady and persistent ever since it started, mostly because my life is still completely up in the air with my lease, student loans, and lack of stable work. I feel like this reaction is directly tied to all this current uncertainty, and I really hope it will improve once I can get my situation sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would start enjoying your activities again and feel more in control if your life situation improved, such as finding a new job or resolving your housing and financial issues?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I keep telling myself that once I sort out a stable job or figure out a new plan, this overwhelming reaction won't go on forever. It really feels directly tied to everything being so up in the air right now, rather than being some kind of long-standing issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your emotional state and inability to cope would persist even after your current life situation improves, or do you think they would significantly improve once your external circumstances change?\n\nPatient: I definitely think things will significantly improve once my circumstances change. I keep telling myself this won't go on forever and that once I sort out a stable job or a concrete plan, I'll be able to cope again. It really feels tied to everything being up in the air right now rather than some permanent, long-standing issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to face another significant setback or stressor in the future, you would likely experience a similar overwhelming reaction, or do you believe you would be able to cope better?\n\nPatient: I believe I would be able to cope much better in the future. This extreme reaction is really just because everything hit me at once—the job falling through, my lease ending, and my student loans coming due. Once I find some stability, I don't think a future setback would derail me like this, as it feels very specific to this overwhelming transition.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you believe you would cope better with future setbacks once you've found stability, but can you tell me more about what you think is different about your current situation that's making it so hard for you to cope, and how you think you would handle a similar situation in the future?"
  }

What makes this current situation so difficult is that everything hit me at once—losing the promised job happened right as my student loans were coming due and my lease was ending, leaving me with absolutely no safety net. I don't really feel guilty, but I just feel completely overwhelmed and frozen by my inability to cope with everything being thrown off course.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling overwhelmed and disconnected from your usual activities since the job opportunity fell through?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, ever since I found out the nonprofit job wasn't happening. All of this really started within a few weeks of getting the news about the hiring freeze, and I've felt unable to cope ever since.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your feelings of being overwhelmed and disconnected from activities have changed or evolved over these past two months, have they been steadily present or have you noticed any fluctuations?\n\nPatient: It has been pretty steady and persistent ever since it started, mostly because my life is still completely up in the air with my lease, student loans, and lack of stable work. I feel like this reaction is directly tied to all this current uncertainty, and I really hope it will improve once I can get my situation sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would start enjoying your activities again and feel more in control if your life situation improved, such as finding a new job or resolving your housing and financial issues?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I keep telling myself that once I sort out a stable job or figure out a new plan, this overwhelming reaction won't go on forever. It really feels directly tied to everything being so up in the air right now, rather than being some kind of long-standing issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your emotional state and inability to cope would persist even after your current life situation improves, or do you think they would significantly improve once your external circumstances change?\n\nPatient: I definitely think things will significantly improve once my circumstances change. I keep telling myself this won't go on forever and that once I sort out a stable job or a concrete plan, I'll be able to cope again. It really feels tied to everything being up in the air right now rather than some permanent, long-standing issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to face another significant setback or stressor in the future, you would likely experience a similar overwhelming reaction, or do you believe you would be able to cope better?\n\nPatient: I believe I would be able to cope much better in the future. This extreme reaction is really just because everything hit me at once—the job falling through, my lease ending, and my student loans coming due. Once I find some stability, I don't think a future setback would derail me like this, as it feels very specific to this overwhelming transition.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you believe you would cope better with future setbacks once you've found stability, but can you tell me more about what you think is different about your current situation that's making it so hard for you to cope, and how you think you would handle a similar situation in the future?\n\nPatient: What makes this current situation so difficult is that everything hit me at once—losing the promised job happened right as my student loans were coming due and my lease was ending, leaving me with absolutely no safety net. In the future, once I have a stable job and a solid plan, I won't have this extreme baseline of insecurity, so a setback wouldn't feel like my entire life is collapsing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current emotional state is a direct result of the accumulation of stressors, such as the job loss, financial issues, and housing uncertainty, or do you feel that there's an underlying issue that's making you more vulnerable to these stressors?\n\nPatient: I definitely feel it's a direct result of all these stressors hitting me at once, rather than any underlying issue. This reaction feels temporary and completely tied to everything being up in the air right now. Once I can secure a stable job or housing plan, I'm confident I will be able to recover and cope normally again."

The patient expresses a belief that their emotional state will improve once their external circumstances change, indicating a strong link between their current situation and their emotional response. This pattern is more suggestive of an adjustment disorder, which is characterized by an emotional or behavioral reaction to a significant stressor, rather than a depressive episode, which might not be as directly tied to external stressors. The patient's anticipation of recovery and ability to cope better in the future with stability also supports this adjustment disorder candidate over a depressive disorder candidate.", "is_final": true}
